    % For linebreaks inside Verbatim environment from package fancyvrb.
    \makeatletter
        \newbox\Wrappedcontinuationbox
        \newbox\Wrappedvisiblespacebox
        \newcommand*\Wrappedvisiblespace {\textcolor{red}{\textvisiblespace}}
        \newcommand*\Wrappedcontinuationsymbol {\textcolor{red}{\llap{\tiny$\m@th\hookrightarrow$}}}
        \newcommand*\Wrappedcontinuationindent {3ex }
        \newcommand*\Wrappedafterbreak {\kern\Wrappedcontinuationindent\copy\Wrappedcontinuationbox}
        % Take advantage of the already applied Pygments mark-up to insert
        % potential linebreaks for TeX processing.
        %        {, <, #, %, $, ' and ": go to next line.
        %        _, }, ^, &, >, - and ~: stay at end of broken line.
        % Use of \textquotesingle for straight quote.
        \newcommand*\Wrappedbreaksatspecials {%
            \def\PYGZus{\discretionary{\char`\_}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\char`\_}}%
            \def\PYGZob{\discretionary{}{\Wrappedafterbreak\char`\{}{\char`\{}}%
            \def\PYGZcb{\discretionary{\char`\}}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\char`\}}}%
            \def\PYGZca{\discretionary{\char`\^}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\char`\^}}%
            \def\PYGZam{\discretionary{\char`\&}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\char`\&}}%
            \def\PYGZlt{\discretionary{}{\Wrappedafterbreak\char`\<}{\char`\<}}%
            \def\PYGZgt{\discretionary{\char`\>}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\char`\>}}%
            \def\PYGZsh{\discretionary{}{\Wrappedafterbreak\char`\#}{\char`\#}}%
            \def\PYGZpc{\discretionary{}{\Wrappedafterbreak\char`\%}{\char`\%}}%
            \def\PYGZdl{\discretionary{}{\Wrappedafterbreak\char`\$}{\char`\$}}%
            \def\PYGZhy{\discretionary{\char`\-}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\char`\-}}%
            \def\PYGZsq{\discretionary{}{\Wrappedafterbreak\textquotesingle}{\textquotesingle}}%
            \def\PYGZdq{\discretionary{}{\Wrappedafterbreak\char`\"}{\char`\"}}%
            \def\PYGZti{\discretionary{\char`\~}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\char`\~}}%
        }
        % Some characters . , ; ? ! / are not pygmentized.
        % This macro makes them "active" and they will insert potential linebreaks
        \newcommand*\Wrappedbreaksatpunct {%
            \lccode`\~`\.\lowercase{\def~}{\discretionary{\hbox{\char`\.}}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\hbox{\char`\.}}}%
            \lccode`\~`\,\lowercase{\def~}{\discretionary{\hbox{\char`\,}}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\hbox{\char`\,}}}%
            \lccode`\~`\;\lowercase{\def~}{\discretionary{\hbox{\char`\;}}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\hbox{\char`\;}}}%
            \lccode`\~`\:\lowercase{\def~}{\discretionary{\hbox{\char`\:}}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\hbox{\char`\:}}}%
            \lccode`\~`\?\lowercase{\def~}{\discretionary{\hbox{\char`\?}}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\hbox{\char`\?}}}%
            \lccode`\~`\!\lowercase{\def~}{\discretionary{\hbox{\char`\!}}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\hbox{\char`\!}}}%
            \lccode`\~`\/\lowercase{\def~}{\discretionary{\hbox{\char`\/}}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\hbox{\char`\/}}}%
            \catcode`\.\active
            \catcode`\,\active
            \catcode`\;\active
            \catcode`\:\active
            \catcode`\?\active
            \catcode`\!\active
            \catcode`\/\active
            \lccode`\~`\~
        }
    \makeatother

    \let\OriginalVerbatim=\Verbatim

    \renewcommand{\Verbatim}[1][1]{%
        %\parskip\z@skip
        \sbox\Wrappedcontinuationbox {\Wrappedcontinuationsymbol}%
        \sbox\Wrappedvisiblespacebox {\FV@SetupFont\Wrappedvisiblespace}%
        \def\FancyVerbFormatLine ##1{\hsize\linewidth
            \vtop{\raggedright\hyphenpenalty\z@\exhyphenpenalty\z@
                \doublehyphendemerits\z@\finalhyphendemerits\z@
                \strut ##1\strut}%
        }%
        % If the linebreak is at a space, the latter will be displayed as visible
        % space at end of first line, and a continuation symbol starts next line.
        % Stretch/shrink are however usually zero for typewriter font.
        \def\FV@Space {%
            \nobreak\hskip\z@ plus\fontdimen3\font minus\fontdimen4\font
            \discretionary{\copy\Wrappedvisiblespacebox}{\Wrappedafterbreak}
            {\kern\fontdimen2\font}%
        }%

        % Allow breaks at special characters using \PYG... macros.
        \Wrappedbreaksatspecials
        % Breaks at punctuation characters . , ; ? ! and / need catcode=\active
        \OriginalVerbatim[#1,codes*=\Wrappedbreaksatpunct]%
    }

\begin{document}
    
    \maketitle
    
    

    
    \section{Assignment 3: From Fashion-MNIST to Real-World Clothing
Classification}\label{assignment-3-from-fashion-mnist-to-real-world-clothing-classification}

\textbf{Research question:} Do image models that outperform classical
baselines on Fashion-MNIST remain useful under distribution shift,
retail-image fine-tuning, and a small personal real-world image holdout?

\subsection{Abstract}\label{abstract}

This project begins with Fashion-MNIST as a controlled benchmark for
comparing classical machine-learning models against image-specific
neural networks. The clean benchmark establishes the main pattern:
models that preserve image structure, especially CNNs, outperform
methods that treat each image as a flattened vector of pixels. I then
test whether that conclusion still holds when the data become less
standardized. The extensions include synthetic distribution shifts,
unsupervised representation learning with an autoencoder, transfer
learning on curated retail product images, and a small personal
real-world image holdout made from phone photos, screenshots, and
archive-like images.

The strongest clean Fashion-MNIST model is \textbf{CNN-small}, with a
test macro F1 of \textbf{0.890}, compared with \textbf{0.824} for
\textbf{PCA + Logistic Regression}. Under shifted test images, the CNN
is also more stable: its average macro F1 drop is \textbf{0.104},
compared with \textbf{0.431} for the PCA pipeline. The real-image
extensions show both the value and the limits of transfer learning. A
curated six-class ResNet18 retail model reaches \textbf{0.917} test
macro F1 on held-out retail images, but the small personal-image holdout
still exposes domain-gap problems caused by backgrounds, crops, multiple
garments, and ambiguous labels. The final sections therefore add
selective prediction and embedding-based retrieval, which turn the model
from a simple classifier into a more realistic human-review pipeline.

\subsection{Dataset note}\label{dataset-note}

Fashion-MNIST is used as the controlled benchmark, not as the only data
source. To address the limitation that Fashion-MNIST is clean, centered,
grayscale, and highly standardized, I augment the analysis with curated
real retail images and then test the trained pipeline on a small
personal real-world image set. The personal set is intentionally treated
as a qualitative stress test rather than a replacement for the main
benchmark, because it is too small and too visually mixed to support a
reliable final accuracy estimate by itself.

\subsection{Key Results}\label{key-results}

Do image-aware models beat flattened-pixel baselines? & CNN-small
vs.~PCA + Logistic Regression & 0.890 vs.~0.824 test macro F1 &
Preserving spatial structure improves image classification. \\
Does the conclusion survive distribution shift? & Six perturbed
Fashion-MNIST test sets & CNN average macro F1 drop: 0.104; PCA pipeline
drop: 0.431 & The CNN is much more robust when images stop looking
perfectly standardized. \\
Can unsupervised learned features help? & Autoencoder latent vectors +
logistic regression & 0.820 clean test macro F1; 0.119 average
shifted-set drop & Learned representations transfer better than purely
linear PCA compression, but remain below the supervised CNN. \\
Can the pipeline adapt to real product photos? & ImageNet-pretrained
ResNet18 on curated six-class retail data & 0.917 retail test macro F1 &
Transfer learning adapts well to a cleaner real-image domain. \\
Does it work on personal archive-like images? & Fine-tuned ResNet18 on
phone photos/screenshots & Qualitative stress test with 10 scored images
plus 1 ambiguous image & The model can ingest personal images, but
backgrounds, crops, and taxonomy gaps still cause errors. \\
How should uncertain predictions be handled? & Selective prediction at
confidence thresholds 0.50, 0.70, 0.85 & Higher thresholds reduce
coverage but increase accepted-image accuracy & A deployment pipeline
should include abstention and human review, not only hard
classification. \\
Can the learned model support another task? & ResNet embedding retrieval
by cosine similarity & Top-5 neighbors often contain the expected class
even when top-1 fails & The same learned representation can support
search and review workflows without retraining. \\
\end{longtable}
}

    \subsection{1. Data Explanation}\label{data-explanation}

This project uses \textbf{Fashion-MNIST} as the primary controlled
benchmark. Fashion-MNIST is a dataset of grayscale clothing images
introduced by Xiao, Rasul, and Vollgraf (2017). The dataset contains:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{60,000} training images
\item
  \texttt{10,000} test images
\item
  \texttt{10} clothing categories
\item
  one \texttt{28\ x\ 28} grayscale image per item
\end{itemize}

The ten classes are:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  T-shirt/top
\item
  Trouser
\item
  Pullover
\item
  Dress
\item
  Coat
\item
  Sandal
\item
  Shirt
\item
  Sneaker
\item
  Bag
\item
  Ankle boot
\end{enumerate}

\subsubsection{Why this dataset was
chosen}\label{why-this-dataset-was-chosen}

Fashion-MNIST provides a clean supervised-learning setting where:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  the labels are already defined,
\item
  the class balance is uniform,
\item
  the visual differences between some classes are obvious and others are
  subtle,
\item
  and both classical models and deep-learning models can be compared
  meaningfully.
\end{itemize}

That makes it useful as a benchmark, but it is not enough by itself. Its
images are centered, low-resolution, grayscale, and drawn from a
consistent distribution. Real clothing images are messier: they have
color, backgrounds, varied poses, partial crops, screenshots, lighting
changes, and sometimes multiple garments in the same image. For that
reason, the later sections extend the benchmark with curated retail
images and a small personal real-world image set.

\subsubsection{How the data was
obtained}\label{how-the-data-was-obtained}

The Fashion-MNIST data is obtained through the \texttt{torchvision}
dataset interface, which downloads the official files and exposes them
as Python objects. The retail-image extension is built from curated
product images mapped back to the Fashion-MNIST clothing vocabulary,
then simplified into six more stable real-image groups. The personal
holdout uses a small set of manually labeled images from my own archive
and is used only as a stress test for domain shift.

\subsubsection{How the sample is formed}\label{how-the-sample-is-formed}

To keep the Fashion-MNIST part of the notebook fast enough to rerun on a
normal laptop CPU, I use:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  a stratified working training sample of \texttt{10,000} images,
\item
  a stratified validation sample of \texttt{2,000} images,
\item
  and the official \texttt{10,000} image test split as the final
  out-of-sample evaluation set.
\end{itemize}

This stratification strategy preserves the original class balance, so
the reduced sample still represents the full Fashion-MNIST dataset
fairly. The retail and personal-image sections use separate metadata
manifests so that those extensions are auditable rather than mixed into
the original benchmark split.

    \subsection{2. Converting the Data to Python-Readable
Format}\label{converting-the-data-to-python-readable-format}

The raw Fashion-MNIST files are stored in IDX format. Instead of parsing
those files manually, this notebook uses
\texttt{torchvision.datasets.FashionMNIST}, which:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  downloads the official files,
\item
  verifies and reads them,
\item
  exposes the pixel grids as tensor-backed arrays,
\item
  and makes the labels available alongside the images.
\end{itemize}

This section is the model's ``data ingestion'' layer, converting
external files into Python data structures that the rest of the analysis
can use.

The code below:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  downloads or loads the dataset,
\item
  converts images to NumPy arrays scaled to \texttt{{[}0,\ 1{]}},
\item
  creates a stratified train/validation split from the official training
  data,
\item
  and prepares both image-shaped arrays and flattened arrays for later
  models.
\end{itemize}

\prompt{In}{incolor}{94}{\boxspacing}
\begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
\PY{n}{data\PYZus{}root} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{Path}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{cwd}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)} \PY{o}{/} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{data}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Load the official Fashion\PYZhy{}MNIST training and test datasets.}
\PY{n}{train\PYZus{}full} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{FashionMNIST}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{root}\PY{o}{=}\PY{n}{data\PYZus{}root}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{train}\PY{o}{=}\PY{k+kc}{True}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{download}\PY{o}{=}\PY{k+kc}{True}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{test\PYZus{}full} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{FashionMNIST}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{root}\PY{o}{=}\PY{n}{data\PYZus{}root}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{train}\PY{o}{=}\PY{k+kc}{False}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{download}\PY{o}{=}\PY{k+kc}{True}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Convert the image data into NumPy arrays and rescale pixels from}
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} integer values in [0, 255] to floating point values in [0, 1].}
\PY{n}{X\PYZus{}full} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{train\PYZus{}full}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{data}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{numpy}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{astype}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{float32}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)} \PY{o}{/} \PY{l+m+mf}{255.0}
\PY{n}{y\PYZus{}full} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{train\PYZus{}full}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{targets}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{numpy}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{X\PYZus{}test} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{test\PYZus{}full}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{data}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{numpy}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{astype}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{float32}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)} \PY{o}{/} \PY{l+m+mf}{255.0}
\PY{n}{y\PYZus{}test} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{test\PYZus{}full}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{targets}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{numpy}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Create a manageable working subset from the official training data.}
\PY{n}{working\PYZus{}indices}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{\PYZus{}} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{train\PYZus{}test\PYZus{}split}\PY{p}{(}
    \PY{n}{np}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{arange}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n+nb}{len}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{y\PYZus{}full}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{,}
    \PY{n}{train\PYZus{}size}\PY{o}{=}\PY{n}{WORKING\PYZus{}TRAIN\PYZus{}SIZE} \PY{o}{+} \PY{n}{WORKING\PYZus{}VAL\PYZus{}SIZE}\PY{p}{,}
    \PY{n}{stratify}\PY{o}{=}\PY{n}{y\PYZus{}full}\PY{p}{,}
    \PY{n}{random\PYZus{}state}\PY{o}{=}\PY{n}{SEED}\PY{p}{,}
\PY{p}{)}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Split that subset into training and validation partitions.}
\PY{n}{train\PYZus{}idx}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{val\PYZus{}idx} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{train\PYZus{}test\PYZus{}split}\PY{p}{(}
    \PY{n}{working\PYZus{}indices}\PY{p}{,}
    \PY{n}{train\PYZus{}size}\PY{o}{=}\PY{n}{WORKING\PYZus{}TRAIN\PYZus{}SIZE}\PY{p}{,}
    \PY{n}{stratify}\PY{o}{=}\PY{n}{y\PYZus{}full}\PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{working\PYZus{}indices}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{,}
    \PY{n}{random\PYZus{}state}\PY{o}{=}\PY{n}{SEED}\PY{p}{,}
\PY{p}{)}

\PY{n}{X\PYZus{}train} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{X\PYZus{}full}\PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{train\PYZus{}idx}\PY{p}{]}
\PY{n}{y\PYZus{}train} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{y\PYZus{}full}\PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{train\PYZus{}idx}\PY{p}{]}
\PY{n}{X\PYZus{}val} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{X\PYZus{}full}\PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{val\PYZus{}idx}\PY{p}{]}
\PY{n}{y\PYZus{}val} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{y\PYZus{}full}\PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{val\PYZus{}idx}\PY{p}{]}

\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} Classical models in scikit\PYZhy{}learn expect tabular feature vectors, so}
\PY{c+c1}{\PYZsh{} we also keep flattened versions of each image.}
\PY{n}{X\PYZus{}train\PYZus{}flat} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{X\PYZus{}train}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{reshape}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n+nb}{len}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{X\PYZus{}train}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{,} \PY{o}{\PYZhy{}}\PY{l+m+mi}{1}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{X\PYZus{}val\PYZus{}flat} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{X\PYZus{}val}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{reshape}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n+nb}{len}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{X\PYZus{}val}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{,} \PY{o}{\PYZhy{}}\PY{l+m+mi}{1}\PY{p}{)}
\PY{n}{X\PYZus{}test\PYZus{}flat} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{X\PYZus{}test}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{reshape}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n+nb}{len}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{X\PYZus{}test}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{,} \PY{o}{\PYZhy{}}\PY{l+m+mi}{1}\PY{p}{)}

\PY{n}{split\PYZus{}summary} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{pd}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{DataFrame}\PY{p}{(}
    \PY{p}{[}
        \PY{p}{\PYZob{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Split}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Train}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Samples}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{n+nb}{len}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{X\PYZus{}train}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Image shape}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{n}{X\PYZus{}train}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{shape}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+m+mi}{1}\PY{p}{:}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{p}{,}
        \PY{p}{\PYZob{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Split}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Validation}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Samples}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{n+nb}{len}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{X\PYZus{}val}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Image shape}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{n}{X\PYZus{}val}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{shape}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+m+mi}{1}\PY{p}{:}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{p}{,}
        \PY{p}{\PYZob{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Split}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Test}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Samples}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{n+nb}{len}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{X\PYZus{}test}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{Image shape}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{n}{X\PYZus{}test}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{shape}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+m+mi}{1}\PY{p}{:}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{p}{,}

\PY{p}{)}

    \begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
        Split  Samples Image shape
0       Train    10000    (28, 28)
1  Validation     2000    (28, 28)
2        Test    10000    (28, 28)
    \end{Verbatim}

    
    At this point the data exists in two parallel forms:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Image tensors} with shape \texttt{(n,\ 28,\ 28)} for the CNN.
\item
  \textbf{Flattened feature vectors} with shape \texttt{(n,\ 784)} for
  classical scikit-learn models.
\end{itemize}

That distinction is important. Flattened vectors ignore spatial
arrangement, while CNNs preserve local neighborhoods of pixels. Much of
the later comparison is really about whether preserving that structure
matters.

    \subsection{3. Cleaning, Pre-Processing, Feature Engineering, and
Exploratory Data
Analysis}\label{cleaning-pre-processing-feature-engineering-and-exploratory-data-analysis}

This dataset is already much cleaner than my first assignment's Toggl
data. There are no missing labels, no duplicate columns, no mixed file
formats, and a very clean distinction between objects. Even so, some
preprocessing is still required.

\subsubsection{Cleaning and preprocessing
choices}\label{cleaning-and-preprocessing-choices}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Pixel scaling:} raw pixel values are scaled from
  \texttt{0-255} to \texttt{0-1}.
\item
  \textbf{Flattening for classical models:} logistic regression and
  Naive Bayes need tabular vectors rather than 2D images.
\item
  \textbf{PCA for dimensionality reduction:} principal component
  analysis is used as a feature-engineering step before one of the
  logistic-regression models.
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Why EDA matters here}\label{why-eda-matters-here}

Before fitting any model, EDA is useful to verify:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  whether the classes are balanced,
\item
  what the images actually look like,
\item
  whether some classes look visually similar,
\item
  and whether the basic summary statistics suggest any obvious issues.
\end{itemize}

    \subsubsection{EDA Takeaways}\label{eda-takeaways}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  The sample is \textbf{perfectly class-balanced} through stratified
  sampling, which preserves the original label distribution.
\item
  Pixel intensities are already on a common scale because values were
  normalized to the interval \texttt{{[}0,\ 1{]}}.
\item
  Some classes are likely to be easy to distinguish visually, such as
  \textbf{bags}, because nothing else in the dataset has the same
  silhouette.
\item
  Other classes may be harder to differentiate because they have
  overlapping silhouettes, such as \textbf{shirts}, \textbf{pullovers},
  and \textbf{coats}. This should make edge and shape information
  especially important for the model.
\end{itemize}

    \subsection{4. Analysis Plan and Data
Splits}\label{analysis-plan-and-data-splits}

For this dataset, the goal is \textbf{multiclass classification}. Each
image belongs to exactly one of ten clothing categories, so the model's
job is to predict the correct class label. This is logical for this
dataset as the target variable is categorical, so regression would be
completely inappropriate, the labels of the data are known in advance.

\subsubsection{Data split strategy}\label{data-split-strategy}

Three splits will be used: - \textbf{Training set:} used to fit model
parameters. - \textbf{Validation set:} used to compare models and tune
CNN hyperparameters. - \textbf{Test set:} used once at the end for
out-of-sample evaluation.

This split is important because a model can appear to perform very well
on the same data it was trained on while failing to generalize to unseen
examples. Separating training, validation, and test data helps reduce
the risk of overfitting, supports more reliable model selection, and
provides a more honest estimate of how well the final classifier will
perform on new images.

    \#\#\# Split Summary

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Training images: \textbf{10,000}
\item
  Validation images: \textbf{2,000}
\item
  Test images: \textbf{10,000}
\item
  Feature dimension for classical models after flattening: \textbf{784}
\end{itemize}

The training and validation sets come from the official Fashion-MNIST
training split, while the test set remains untouched until the final
evaluation stage.

    \subsection{5. Model Selection and Mathematical
Background}\label{model-selection-and-mathematical-background}

I compare several models to better answer the research question as it
better shows the progression of accuracy by the more advanced
architecture. The sequence moves from a trivial baseline, to simple
probabilistic and linear models, and finally to a convolutional neural
network that is better suited to image structure.

\subsubsection{Models included}\label{models-included}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Majority-class baseline}\\
\item
  \textbf{Gaussian Naive Bayes}\\
\item
  \textbf{Logistic regression}\\
\item
  \textbf{PCA + logistic regression}\\
\item
  \textbf{A small CNN}
\end{enumerate}

\subsubsection{5.1 Majority-class
baseline}\label{majority-class-baseline}

This model always predicts the most common class in the training data,
regardless of the input image. It does not learn any relationship
between pixel values and clothing labels, so it serves only as a minimum
benchmark. The purpose of such a baseline is to establish a floor for
performance, so if a more advanced model cannot outperform this
baseline, then the learned model is not extracting useful structure from
the data.

\subsubsection{5.2 Gaussian Naive Bayes}\label{gaussian-naive-bayes}

Naive Bayes predicts a class by combining a prior belief about how
common each class is with evidence from each feature:

\[
p(y \mid x_1, \dots, x_d) \propto p(y) \prod_{j=1}^{d} p(x_j \mid y)
\]

Here, \(y\) is the class label, and \(x_1, \dots, x_d\) are the input
features, where each feature corresponds to one pixel value. The term
\(p(y)\) is the \textbf{prior probability} of class \(y\), meaning how
likely that class is before looking at the image. The term
\(p(x_j \mid y)\) is the \textbf{likelihood} of observing pixel value
\(x_j\) if the image truly belongs to class \(y\). The product over all
\(d\) features combines the evidence from every pixel.

The proportionality symbol means the right-hand side is not yet
normalized into a proper probability distribution. After computing this
quantity for each class, the model compares the values and chooses the
class with the largest posterior probability.

What makes this Gaussian is the assumption for each feature to follow a
normal distribution. So for each class, the model summarizes each pixel
using a mean and variance. This is computationally simple, but it treats
each pixel as conditionally independent of every other pixel once the
class is known. For image data, this is clearly unrealistic, since
neighboring pixels are often highly related. Still, Gaussian Naive Bayes
is useful as a fast probabilistic baseline because it shows how far one
can get with very simple distributional assumptions.

\subsubsection{5.3 Multinomial logistic
regression}\label{multinomial-logistic-regression}

Logistic regression is a discriminative linear classifier. For
multiclass prediction, it estimates the probability of each class using
the softmax function:

\[
p(y = k \mid x) = \frac{\exp(w_k^T x + b_k)}{\sum_{j=1}^{K} \exp(w_j^T x + b_j)}
\]

In this equation, \(x\) is the input vector of pixel values, \(K\) is
the total number of classes, \(w_k\) is the weight vector for class
\(k\), and \(b_k\) is the bias term for class \(k\). The quantity
\(w_k^T x + b_k\) is the \textbf{score} the model assigns to class
\(k\). It is a weighted sum of the input features plus an offset.
Intuitively, the weights tell the model which pixels push the prediction
toward or away from a given class.

The exponential function ensures that all class scores become positive.
The denominator sums these exponentiated scores across all classes,
which normalizes the output so the predicted probabilities add up to 1.
This lets the model output a full probability distribution over the ten
clothing categories.

As a whole, the softmax equation turns a set of raw linear scores into
interpretable class probabilities. The model learns the weights and
biases that make the correct class score highest for training examples.
Compared with Naive Bayes, logistic regression is stronger because it
does not assume pixel independence. However, it is still a
\textbf{linear} model, so its ability to capture more complex image
patterns is limited.

\subsubsection{5.4 Principal component analysis
(PCA)}\label{principal-component-analysis-pca}

PCA transforms the original input into a smaller set of derived
features:

\[
z = W^T x
\]

Here, \(x\) is the original high-dimensional input vector, \(W\) is a
matrix whose columns are the principal components, and \(z\) is the
lower-dimensional representation of the data after projection. Each
column of \(W\) defines a direction in feature space along which the
data vary strongly. The transpose \(W^T\) projects the original data
onto those directions.

PCA replaces the original pixel basis with a new coordinate system that
captures the most important variation in the dataset. Instead of using
every raw pixel directly, the model uses a smaller number of summary
dimensions that preserve as much variance as possible.

As a whole, PCA serves two purposes here. First, it reduces
dimensionality, which can make downstream models faster and less noisy.
Second, it can improve generalization if the discarded directions mostly
contain redundancy or noise. However, PCA preserves directions of high
variance, not necessarily directions that are most useful for
classification. So for this assignment, PCA + logistic regression tests
whether a more compact representation helps performance or whether
useful class information is lost during compression.

\subsubsection{5.5 Convolutional neural network
(CNN)}\label{convolutional-neural-network-cnn}

A convolutional layer computes feature maps by applying learned filters
to local regions of the image:

\[
h^{(l)}_{i,j,c} = \sigma\left((W_c * x^{(l-1)})_{i,j} + b_c\right)
\]

In this expression, \(x^{(l-1)}\) is the input to layer \(l\), \(W_c\)
is the filter for channel \(c\), \(*\) denotes convolution, \(b_c\) is a
bias term, and \(\sigma\) is a nonlinear activation function such as
ReLU. The output \(h^{(l)}_{i,j,c}\) is the activation at spatial
position \((i,j)\) in channel \(c\) of layer \(l\).

The convolution operation slides the filter across the image and
computes how strongly that filter matches each local patch. If a filter
has learned to detect something like an edge, corner, or texture, then
it will produce a strong response in regions where that pattern appears.
The bias term shifts the pre-activation value, and the nonlinearity
allows the network to learn more complex functions than a purely linear
system could represent.

As a whole, this equation shows how CNNs build structured image
representations from local patterns. This is especially appropriate for
the Fashion-MNIST dataset because nearby pixels to show shapes, edges,
seams, and outlines, which are combined as spatial patterns. Unlike
Naive Bayes or logistic regression on flattened pixels, a CNN can
exploit this local structure.

pseudocode}\label{training-algorithm-in-pseudocode}

```text for each epoch: for each minibatch: compute model outputs
compute loss backpropagate gradients update parameters with Adam
evaluate on validation set keep the checkpoint with the best validation
macro F1

    \subsection{6. Model Training, Cross Validation, and Hyperparameter
Tuning}\label{model-training-cross-validation-and-hyperparameter-tuning}

This section fits the models and compares them on the validation set.

\subsubsection{Training strategy}\label{training-strategy}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  The classical models are fit once on the training split and evaluated
  on validation and test data.
\item
  The CNN is trained with mini-batch gradient descent using
  \textbf{Adam}.
\item
  The CNN search compares two small architectures that differ in
  hidden-layer width and dropout.
\item
  The best CNN checkpoint is selected by \textbf{validation macro F1}
  rather than training loss.
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Why macro F1?}\label{why-macro-f1}

The \textbf{F1 score} combines \textbf{precision} and \textbf{recall}
into a single, comparable, number. Precision measures, out of the
examples predicted as a class, how many were actually correct. Recall
measures, out of the examples that truly belong to a class, how many the
model successfully found. The F1 score is the harmonic mean of precision
and recall given by:

\[
F1 = 2 \cdot \frac{\text{precision} \cdot \text{recall}}{\text{precision} + \text{recall}}
\]

A model can only get a high F1 score when it performs well on
\textbf{both} precision and recall. If one is low, the F1 score will
also be low.

In a multiclass setting, there is one F1 score for each class.
\textbf{Macro F1} computes the F1 score separately for each class and
then takes the simple average across classes. Every class contributes
equally to the final score, regardless of how frequent that class is in
the dataset.

Macro F1 is a useful comparison metric for the task at hand because
plain accuracy can be misleading. A model can achieve high accuracy by
doing very well on easier or more common classes while still performing
poorly on harder classes. Since this project compares performance across
ten clothing categories, the evaluation should not be dominated by the
classes the model finds easiest. Using macro F1 makes the selection
criterion more sensitive to balanced performance across all categories.
Thus, a \textbf{validation macro F1} is used to choose the best CNN
checkpoint because it better reflects whether the model is learning to
classify the full set of clothing categories well, rather than simply
minimizing loss or maximizing overall accuracy.

    \begin{center}
    \adjustimage{max size={0.9\linewidth}{0.9\paperheight}}{final_nbconvert_files/final_nbconvert_15_0.png}
    \end{center}
    { \hspace*{\fill} \\}
    
    \begin{Verbatim}[commandchars=\\\{\}]
Using 100 PCA components retains approximately 0.873 of the training variance.
    \end{Verbatim}

    The PCA explained-variance graph shows that a relatively small number of
principal components captures most of the meaningful variation in the
Fashion-MNIST images. In this notebook, using 100 components retains
about 87.3\% of the variance in the training data, which suggests that
the original 784 pixel features contain substantial redundancy. This
means the images can be compressed into a lower-dimensional
representation without losing too much information, helping the logistic
regression model train faster while still preserving strong predictive
performance.

\PY{+w}{    }\PY{l+s+sd}{\PYZdq{}\PYZdq{}\PYZdq{}Evaluates a CNN on a loader and returns the loss plus classification metrics.\PYZdq{}\PYZdq{}\PYZdq{}}
    \PY{n}{model}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{eval}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}

    \PY{n}{total\PYZus{}loss} \PY{o}{=} \PY{l+m+mf}{0.0}
    \PY{n}{criterion} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{nn}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{CrossEntropyLoss}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}

    \PY{k}{with} \PY{n}{torch}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{no\PYZus{}grad}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{:}
        \PY{k}{for} \PY{n}{batch\PYZus{}inputs}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{batch\PYZus{}targets} \PY{o+ow}{in} \PY{n}{data\PYZus{}loader}\PY{p}{:}

\PY{k}{def} \PY{n+nf}{train\PYZus{}cnn}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{config}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{epochs}\PY{o}{=}\PY{n}{EPOCHS}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{patience}\PY{o}{=}\PY{n}{PATIENCE}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{:}

    \PY{n}{model} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{FashionCNN}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{hidden\PYZus{}dim}\PY{o}{=}\PY{n}{config}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{hidden\PYZus{}dim}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{dropout}\PY{o}{=}\PY{n}{config}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{dropout}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{)}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{to}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{DEVICE}\PY{p}{)}
    \PY{n}{optimizer} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{torch}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{optim}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{Adam}\PY{p}{(}

    \PY{p}{)}
    \PY{n}{criterion} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{nn}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{CrossEntropyLoss}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}

    \PY{n}{best\PYZus{}state} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{copy}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{deepcopy}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{model}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{state\PYZus{}dict}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{)}
    \PY{n}{best\PYZus{}val\PYZus{}f1} \PY{o}{=} \PY{o}{\PYZhy{}}\PY{n}{np}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{inf}

    \PY{k}{for} \PY{n}{epoch} \PY{o+ow}{in} \PY{n+nb}{range}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+m+mi}{1}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{epochs} \PY{o}{+} \PY{l+m+mi}{1}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{:}

            \PY{n}{batch\PYZus{}inputs} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{batch\PYZus{}inputs}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{to}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{DEVICE}\PY{p}{)}

            \PY{n}{optimizer}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{zero\PYZus{}grad}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}

    \begin{center}
    \adjustimage{max size={0.9\linewidth}{0.9\paperheight}}{final_nbconvert_files/final_nbconvert_18_1.png}
    \end{center}
    { \hspace*{\fill} \\}
    
    The CNN comparison shows that both architectures performed well, but the
smaller CNN was the better model overall. CNN-small achieved a higher
validation macro F1 than CNN-large, suggesting that increasing model
size did not improve generalization. Similarly, the learning-curve graph
shows that training loss decreases steadily while validation loss
stabilizes, indicating that the model is learning useful structure
without substantial additional gains from a larger architecture. This
suggests a modest CNN is expressive enough to capture the important
local image patterns in the Fashion-MNIST dataset, while a larger
architecture adds complexity without improving validation performance.

The test set is intentionally not used at this stage. Model selection is
based only on validation results so that the final test evaluation
remains a fair estimate of how well the chosen model generalizes to
unseen data.

    \subsection{7. Out-of-Sample Predictions and Performance
Metrics}\label{out-of-sample-predictions-and-performance-metrics}

The models are now applied to the \textbf{held-out test set} to measure
how well the models generalize to images they were never trained on.

\subsubsection{Metrics used}\label{metrics-used}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Accuracy:} the fraction of all predictions that are correct.
\item
  \textbf{Macro F1:} the average F1 score across classes, giving each
  class equal weight.
\end{itemize}

Macro F1 is especially useful here because some classes are harder than
others, and a model that does well only on easy classes would be
misleading.

    \subsection{8. Visualizing the Results and Discussing
Conclusions}\label{visualizing-the-results-and-discussing-conclusions}

Although the summary metric table provides an important overview of
model performance, it does not fully explain \textbf{how} the models are
making mistakes. For that reason, the visualizations below are used to
examine the results in more detail. In particular, they help reveal
which class pairs are most often confused, whether the CNN corrects
errors that the classical models tend to make, and whether the remaining
mistakes are sensible given the visual similarity of certain clothing
categories.

These questions matter because strong machine-learning analysis is not
only about reporting a final score. It is also about interpreting the
structure of the errors, understanding what the model has actually
learned, and identifying the limits of its performance. Looking at
confusion patterns therefore gives a more complete picture of model
quality than accuracy or macro F1 alone.

    \subsubsection{Results Discussion}\label{results-discussion}

The results show a clear performance hierarchy across the models. Among
the classical approaches, \textbf{PCA + Logistic Regression} performed
best, achieving a test macro F1 of \textbf{0.824}. This suggests that
reducing the dimensionality of the raw pixel space before classification
helped the linear model focus on the most informative variation in the
images. However, the strongest overall model was \textbf{CNN-small},
which achieved a test macro F1 of \textbf{0.890}. This outperformed the
best classical baseline by \textbf{0.066}, indicating that the CNN was
better able to capture the local spatial patterns that matter for image
recognition.

The improvement over the \textbf{majority-class baseline} is even
larger: \textbf{0.872} in macro F1. This confirms that the final model
is learning substantial structure from the data rather than relying on
class frequency alone. More broadly, the results support the expectation
that architectures designed for images should outperform models that
treat the input as a flat vector of independent features.

The error plots suggest that the remaining mistakes are concentrated
among visually similar upper-body garments, especially \textbf{shirts},
\textbf{pullovers}, and \textbf{coats}. This pattern is intuitive, since
these categories often share overlapping shapes, silhouettes, and
textures in low-resolution grayscale images. That makes the remaining
errors meaningful rather than random, and it supports the interpretation
that the CNN is learning genuine visual structure in the dataset rather
than memorizing arbitrary patterns.

\subsubsection{Confusion Matrix
Interpretation}\label{confusion-matrix-interpretation}

The confusion matrices show that the hardest part of this task is not
telling very different items apart, but distinguishing between
\textbf{visually similar clothing categories}. The most common errors
are concentrated among upper-body garments such as \textbf{T-shirt/top,
shirt, pullover, and coat}. This is especially clear in the classical
model, where categories like \textbf{shirt → T-shirt/top},
\textbf{pullover → coat}, and \textbf{coat → pullover} are confused
frequently. The CNN improves performance overall, but its remaining
mistakes are still concentrated in these same categories, which suggests
that these classes are genuinely difficult to separate.

This pattern makes intuitive sense. In \textbf{28 × 28 grayscale
images}, many of the details that distinguish these garments --- such as
sleeve shape, collar structure, or fabric thickness, are hard to see
clearly, even to the naked eye. By contrast, classes with more
distinctive shapes, such as \textbf{trousers, bags, sandals, and ankle
boots}, are classified much more reliably. The main shoe-related
confusion that remains is \textbf{sneaker vs ankle boot}, rather than a
broad failure across all shoe categories.

The misclassified-image grid helps explain these results visually.
Instead of showing just counts, it displays individual test examples the
CNN got wrong, making it possible to judge whether the errors are
understandable. In many cases, they are: several of the incorrect
predictions look genuinely ambiguous at this resolution. Together, these
visualizations suggest that the CNN is learning meaningful image
structure, but that its remaining errors are concentrated in
fine-grained categories with overlapping silhouettes rather than being
random mistakes.

    \subsection{8.1 Robustness to Archive-Like Distribution Shift and
Representation
Learning}\label{robustness-to-archive-like-distribution-shift-and-representation-learning}

Because Fashion-MNIST is highly standardized, I tested whether the main
results still hold when the test images are perturbed in ways that
better resemble a personal archive. I created shifted versions of the
held-out test set using small translations, slight rotations, Gaussian
noise, blur, brightness and contrast changes, and crop-and-pad
distortions. This extension addresses the notebook's main limitation
directly: the benchmark is useful for controlled comparison, but
real-world image data rarely remain as clean, centered, and uniform as
Fashion-MNIST.

The overall pattern remained clear. On the clean benchmark,
\textbf{CNN-small} still outperformed \textbf{PCA + Logistic
Regression}, but both models lost performance once the images stopped
looking perfectly standardized. Across the six shifted test sets, the
average macro F1 drop was \textbf{0.104} for \textbf{CNN-small} and
\textbf{0.431} for \textbf{PCA + Logistic Regression}, showing that the
CNN was substantially more robust under distribution shift. The hardest
perturbation for \textbf{CNN-small} in this notebook was
\textbf{Translate (+2,+2)}, where its macro F1 fell to \textbf{0.657}.
Even so, the CNN remained much more stable overall, which suggests that
spatially aware models generalize better once the data begin to depart
from the ideal benchmark format.

    \subsection{8.2 Autoencoder Representation
Learning}\label{autoencoder-representation-learning}

This section adds a second feature-learning approach by training a small
\textbf{convolutional autoencoder} and then using its latent vectors as
inputs to logistic regression. The goal is to test whether an
\textbf{unsupervised learned representation} can support downstream
classification without relying only on PCA or full end-to-end supervised
CNN training. Unlike PCA, which performs a fixed linear compression of
the input, the autoencoder learns a nonlinear representation directly
from the image data itself.

The autoencoder is trained to minimize \textbf{reconstruction error}:

\[
\mathcal{L}_{\text{recon}} = \frac{1}{n} \sum_{i=1}^{n} \|x_i - \hat{x}_i\|_2^2
\]

In this objective, \(n\) is the number of training examples, \(x_i\) is
the original input image, and \(\hat{x}_i\) is the reconstruction
produced by the autoencoder after the image is compressed into a latent
representation and then decoded back into pixel space. The term
\(\|x_i - \hat{x}_i\|_2^2\) measures the squared difference between the
original image and its reconstruction, so it is large when important
visual information is lost and small when the reconstruction is close to
the input.

As a whole, this loss function encourages the encoder to learn a compact
latent space that preserves the main visual structure of the image. In
other words, the model is rewarded for keeping the information needed to
reconstruct shapes, edges, and coarse textures while discarding some of
the redundancy in the raw 784-pixel input. Because the autoencoder is
trained \textbf{without labels}, it is not directly learning to separate
classes; instead, it is learning a compressed representation of the
image distribution itself.

The results suggest that this learned representation is useful for
classification. The latent-feature classifier achieved solid test
performance and was noticeably more stable than the PCA pipeline under
several perturbations, although it still did not match the full CNN.
This places the autoencoder in an interesting middle ground: it performs
better than hand-engineered linear compression under distribution shift,
but it remains weaker than a supervised CNN whose features are optimized
directly for classification. Taken together, these results strengthen
the notebook's main conclusion: once the problem becomes less
standardized, \textbf{representation learning matters}, and models that
preserve or learn image structure have a clearer advantage over methods
that operate only on flattened pixels.

\PY{k}{def} \PY{n+nf}{train\PYZus{}autoencoder}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{epochs}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mi}{6}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{patience}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mi}{2}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{latent\PYZus{}dim}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+m+mi}{64}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{:}
    \PY{n}{model} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{FashionAutoencoder}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{latent\PYZus{}dim}\PY{o}{=}\PY{n}{latent\PYZus{}dim}\PY{p}{)}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{to}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{DEVICE}\PY{p}{)}

    \PY{n}{best\PYZus{}val\PYZus{}loss} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{np}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{inf}

        \PY{n}{model}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{train}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}

        \PY{k}{for} \PY{n}{batch\PYZus{}inputs}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{\PYZus{}} \PY{o+ow}{in} \PY{n}{train\PYZus{}loader}\PY{p}{:}

            \PY{n}{optimizer}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{zero\PYZus{}grad}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}

        \PY{k}{with} \PY{n}{torch}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{no\PYZus{}grad}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{:}

                \PY{n}{reconstructions} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{model}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{batch\PYZus{}inputs}\PY{p}{)}

        \PY{n}{history}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{append}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{\PYZob{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{epoch}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{n}{epoch}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{train\PYZus{}loss}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{n}{train\PYZus{}loss}\PY{p}{,} \PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{l+s+s1}{val\PYZus{}loss}\PY{l+s+s1}{\PYZsq{}}\PY{p}{:} \PY{n}{val\PYZus{}loss}\PY{p}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{p}{)}

            \PY{n}{best\PYZus{}val\PYZus{}loss} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{val\PYZus{}loss}
            \PY{n}{best\PYZus{}state} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{copy}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{deepcopy}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{model}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{state\PYZus{}dict}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{)}
            \PY{n}{best\PYZus{}epoch} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{epoch}

        \PY{k}{else}\PY{p}{:}

            \PY{n}{LogisticRegression}\PY{p}{(}

\PY{p}{)}

    \subsubsection{Autoencoder takeaway}\label{autoencoder-takeaway}

The latent-feature classifier achieves a test macro F1 of
\textbf{0.820}, which places it close to the \textbf{PCA + Logistic
Regression} baseline and below the full \textbf{CNN}. On the clean
benchmark, this means the autoencoder learned a compact representation
that preserves substantial class-relevant information, even though it
was trained without labels. Its best validation reconstruction loss of
\textbf{0.0171}, reached at epoch \textbf{6}, also suggests that the
encoder was able to capture the main visual structure of the
Fashion-MNIST images efficiently.

The more important result appears under distribution shift. Across the
perturbed test sets, the latent-feature classifier's average macro F1
drop is \textbf{0.119}, which is far smaller than the drop for the PCA
pipeline and only modestly worse than the CNN. This suggests that the
autoencoder's learned latent space is more robust than a purely linear
PCA representation once the images stop looking perfectly centered and
standardized. In other words, the autoencoder seems to capture visual
regularities that transfer better beyond the clean benchmark, even
though it was not trained directly for classification.

The practical takeaway is that \textbf{representation learning matters}.
A model does not need to be end-to-end supervised to learn features that
are useful for downstream prediction. However, the results also show the
limit of unsupervised compression: because the autoencoder is trained to
reconstruct images rather than separate classes, it still does not match
the CNN, whose features are optimized specifically for classification.
Taken together, this places the autoencoder in an interesting middle
ground: it is more flexible and robust than hand-engineered linear
compression, but less task-effective than a fully supervised image
model.

    \subsection{8.3 Fine-Tuning on Curated Real Retail
Images}\label{fine-tuning-on-curated-real-retail-images}

The Fashion-MNIST experiments above use clean, centered, grayscale
images. To test whether the modeling approach transfers to real product
photos, I added a curated retailer-image dataset and mapped it back to
the Fashion-MNIST label vocabulary.

The original 10 Fashion-MNIST labels are too granular for noisy retailer
photos. In particular, \texttt{T-shirt/top}, \texttt{Shirt}, and
\texttt{Pullover} overlap heavily, and the footwear classes become
unreliable when segmentation crops remove part of the shoe. For the main
real-image experiment, I therefore use \textbf{stable prepared copies of
raw full product images} and simplify the label space to six more stable
groups: \texttt{top}, \texttt{trouser}, \texttt{dress},
\texttt{outerwear}, \texttt{footwear}, and \texttt{bag}.

The experiment compares two ImageNet-pretrained transfer-learning
backbones: \textbf{ResNet18} and \textbf{MobileNetV3-Small}. ResNet18 is
a standard strong baseline, while MobileNetV3 is smaller and faster. The
goal is not to replace the clean Fashion-MNIST benchmark, but to test
whether the representation can adapt to a noisier real-image version of
the task.

\subsubsection{Transfer-learning
objective}\label{transfer-learning-objective}

The fine-tuned retail model can be written as a pretrained image
representation followed by a learned classification head:

\[
\hat{p}(y=k \mid x) = \mathrm{softmax}(W g_\theta(x) + b)_k
\]

Here, \(g_\theta(x)\) is the feature vector produced by the pretrained
image backbone, such as ResNet18, and \(W\) and \(b\) are the parameters
of the final classification layer. This equation matters because the
model is not learning visual features from scratch. It starts from
ImageNet features that already encode edges, textures, shapes, and
object parts, then adjusts those features and the final head to the six
clothing classes.

The model is trained with multiclass cross-entropy:

\[
\mathcal{L}_{\text{CE}} = -\frac{1}{n} \sum_{i=1}^{n} \log \hat{p}(y_i \mid x_i)
\]

This loss is small when the model assigns high probability to the
correct class and large when it assigns low probability to the correct
class. Statistically, it is the negative log-likelihood of the observed
labels under the model's predicted class probabilities. That makes it
appropriate for this section because the task is supervised multiclass
classification on real images.

    \subsubsection{Simplified Real-Image Experiment
Design}\label{simplified-real-image-experiment-design}

The main real-image experiment uses
\texttt{splits\_raw\_full\_simplified6.csv}. This split is balanced by
the six simplified classes, not by the original ten Fashion-MNIST
classes, and all paths point to prepared copies of raw full images. That
avoids the destructive footwear segmentation problem while still
preserving the original \texttt{fashion\_mnist\_class} column for
auditing.

The training script supports
\texttt{-\/-label-column\ simplified\_class} and
\texttt{-\/-model\ resnet18\textbar{}mobilenet\_v3\_small}. Smoke tests
are only pipeline checks. Full runs are the metrics to report.

\subsubsection{Segmentation note}\label{segmentation-note}

Segmentation was tried as a preprocessing step because, in theory,
removing people and backgrounds should make the model focus more
directly on the garment. In practice, the segmentation masks were not
reliable enough for this dataset. Some outputs removed sleeves, straps,
shoe edges, or other class-relevant chunks, and footwear was especially
sensitive to those errors. For that reason, the final simplified-label
experiment uses raw/full prepared images rather than segmented crops.
This keeps background and pose variation in the data, but it avoids
training or reporting on visibly broken garment images.

    \subsubsection{Simplified Real-Image Results
Loader}\label{simplified-real-image-results-loader}

The table below loads any available simplified-label runs. Prefer the
two full directories for final reporting. Smoke directories are included
only to prove that the pipeline is wired correctly.

            \PY{n}{row}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{worst\PYZus{}class}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{class\PYZus{}rows}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{sort\PYZus{}values}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{f1\PYZhy{}score}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{iloc}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+m+mi}{0}\PY{p}{]}\PY{p}{[}\PY{n}{label\PYZus{}col}\PY{p}{]}
            \PY{n}{row}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{worst\PYZus{}class\PYZus{}f1}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]} \PY{o}{=} \PY{n}{class\PYZus{}rows}\PY{p}{[}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{f1\PYZhy{}score}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{]}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{min}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}
    \PY{k}{return} \PY{n}{row}

\PY{n}{simplified\PYZus{}result\PYZus{}dirs} \PY{o}{=} \PY{p}{[}

    \subsubsection{Manual Curation Loop}\label{manual-curation-loop}

After the first full simplified-label run, use the confusion matrix and
per-class F1 scores to identify the worst simplified groups. The likely
candidates are \texttt{top}, \texttt{outerwear}, and \texttt{footwear}.
For those groups, generate contact sheets from the raw images with
\texttt{scripts/make\_simplified6\_contact\_sheets.py}, remove images
that are mislabeled or visually ambiguous, then regenerate the curated
split and retrain the better backbone.

Rejected examples should be preserved for audit under
\texttt{data/retail\_images/rejected/simplified6/}, with a manifest such
as
\texttt{data/retail\_images/metadata/simplified6\_curation\_rejects.csv}.
The curated retraining split should be named
\texttt{splits\_raw\_full\_simplified6\_curated.csv} so that the
before/after comparison stays explicit.

    \subsection{8.4 Testing the Fine-Tuned Model on Real-World
Images}\label{testing-the-fine-tuned-model-on-real-world-images}

The curated retail experiment above still uses product-style images
collected through a controlled pipeline. As a final domain-shift check,
I test the best simplified-label transfer-learning model on a small set
of personal real-world images from outside that pipeline. These images
include phone photos, screenshots, varied backgrounds, partial crops,
and people wearing multiple garments.

This is not a statistically reliable benchmark: the sample is too small
and several images contain more than one clothing item. I therefore
treat it as a qualitative holdout spot check. For the quantitative
summary, each image is labeled by the \textbf{dominant visible garment}
when it fits one of the six simplified classes from the retail
experiment: \texttt{top}, \texttt{trouser}, \texttt{dress},
\texttt{outerwear}, \texttt{footwear}, and \texttt{bag}. Images that are
ambiguous or outside this taxonomy are marked \texttt{unidentified};
they are still shown in the qualitative table, but they are excluded
from accuracy and macro F1.

This section is important for ML flexibility because it applies the
pipeline to images that were not part of the original Fashion-MNIST
benchmark or the curated retail scrape. The personal holdout is
deliberately modest, but it directly tests whether the trained workflow
can ingest archive-like images with real-world noise.

    \begin{center}
    \adjustimage{max size={0.9\linewidth}{0.9\paperheight}}{final_nbconvert_files/final_nbconvert_41_1.png}
    \end{center}
    { \hspace*{\fill} \\}
    
    The real-world results should be interpreted as a stress test rather
than a replacement for the held-out retail test set. These photos differ
from catalog images in several ways that make the task harder: people
and backgrounds are visible, crops are inconsistent, screenshots
introduce interface artifacts, and some examples contain multiple
garments. The six-class label space also compresses many visual
distinctions, so ambiguous examples such as rompers are marked
\texttt{unidentified} and excluded from scored metrics, while
hoodie/pullover-like garments are scored as \texttt{top} under the
simplified label mapping.

The most useful evidence from this section is therefore not only the
small-sample accuracy, but also the pattern of confidence.
High-confidence correct predictions suggest that the ImageNet-pretrained
retail model learned transferable garment cues. High-confidence mistakes
point to remaining domain-gap issues that would require a larger labeled
real-world set, more augmentation, or labels designed for outfit-level
images rather than isolated products.

    \subsection{8.5 Confidence, Human Review, and Selective
Prediction}\label{confidence-human-review-and-selective-prediction}

The real-world spot check above asks whether the transfer-learning model
can classify images from outside the curated retail pipeline. A
practical deployment question is slightly different: \textbf{when should
the model be trusted, and when should a human review the image instead?}

This section uses the model's top predicted probability as a simple
confidence score. I compare three acceptance thresholds: \texttt{0.50},
\texttt{0.70}, and \texttt{0.85}. Images above the threshold are
accepted automatically; images below it are routed to human review. The
\texttt{unidentified} examples remain visible because they are important
deployment failures, but they are excluded from scored accuracy and
macro F1 because they are outside the six-class model vocabulary.

Mathematically, for a confidence threshold \(\tau\), the automatically
accepted set is:

\[
A_\tau = \{i : \max_k \hat{p}_{ik} \ge \tau\}
\]

where \(\hat{p}_{ik}\) is the model's predicted probability that image
\(i\) belongs to class \(k\). The coverage at that threshold is:

\[
\mathrm{coverage}_\tau = \frac{|A_\tau|}{n}
\]

and the accepted-image accuracy is:

\[
\mathrm{accepted\ accuracy}_\tau = \frac{1}{|A_\tau|} \sum_{i \in A_\tau} \mathbf{1}(\hat{y}_i = y_i)
\]

This separates two questions that ordinary accuracy combines: how many
images the model is willing to classify automatically, and how often
those accepted predictions are correct. That distinction matters for
real-world image data because abstaining on uncertain or out-of-taxonomy
images can be more useful than forcing a label.

    The selective-prediction table shows the practical tradeoff between
automation and caution. Lower thresholds classify more images
automatically, while higher thresholds send more images to review. This
is useful because the model's real-world mistakes are not only ordinary
class errors: some are taxonomy problems where the six trained classes
are not expressive enough.

This extension connects directly to the course material. Sessions 9 and
40 focus on model quality and metrics; this section goes beyond one
accuracy number by separating coverage from accepted-image accuracy.
Session 11 raises the importance of data design and bias; the
\texttt{unidentified} examples show how a label taxonomy can determine
what counts as a valid prediction. Sessions 17 and 18 explain why
convolutional features and transfer learning can work well on images,
but the review table shows that representation learning still needs a
human-facing decision rule when the deployment data are messy.

    \subsection{8.6 ML Flexibility I: Visual Retrieval with ResNet
Embeddings}\label{ml-flexibility-i-visual-retrieval-with-resnet-embeddings}

The fine-tuned ResNet does not have to be used only as a classifier. The
layer before the final class head is also a learned visual
representation. In this section, I reuse that representation for a
different task: \textbf{image retrieval}. For each real-world image, the
pipeline finds the nearest images in the curated retail dataset by
cosine similarity in ResNet embedding space.

This is a practical modern ML pattern. Search, recommendation, duplicate
detection, and human-review tools often use embeddings first, then let a
person or a downstream model interpret the nearest neighbors. Here,
retrieval asks whether the model's learned representation groups
visually similar garments together even when the real-world image is
messy or outside the exact six-class taxonomy.

For a real-world query image \(q\) and a retail reference image \(r\),
cosine similarity is:

\[
s(q, r) = \frac{z_q^T z_r}{\|z_q\|_2 \|z_r\|_2}
\]

where \(z_q = g_\theta(q)\) and \(z_r = g_\theta(r)\) are the ResNet
embeddings. The numerator measures how aligned the two embedding vectors
are, while the denominator normalizes by vector length. This means
retrieval is based on direction in feature space rather than raw
magnitude, which is useful when the goal is visual similarity rather
than classification confidence.

    The retrieval output changes the interpretation of the ResNet from a
closed classifier into a reusable representation model. Instead of only
returning one label, the system can show visually similar retail
examples next to a real-world query. That is useful for human review: a
reviewer can see whether the model is comparing the image to plausible
neighbors, and errors become easier to diagnose.

This is an ML-flexibility extension because the same learned features
are reused for a new task without retraining. It connects to the
syllabus topics on convolutions and transfer learning, while also moving
toward a state-of-the-art pipeline pattern: embedding once, then using
nearest-neighbor search for retrieval, recommendation, and review
workflows.

A limitation of this retrieval view is that the embedding space does not
isolate the garment perfectly. Some nearest neighbors appear to share
background, pose, color palette, or catalog-photo style rather than only
the target clothing item. That does not make retrieval useless; it
actually helps diagnose what the representation is attending to. But it
means the retrieved neighbors should be treated as review evidence, not
as proof that the ResNet has learned a pure garment-shape
representation.

    \subsection{8.7 ML Flexibility II: Optional Zero-Shot Classification
with Image-Text
Models}\label{ml-flexibility-ii-optional-zero-shot-classification-with-image-text-models}

The retrieval section reused the fine-tuned ResNet representation. A
second modern pipeline tests a different kind of flexibility:
\textbf{zero-shot image-text classification}. Models such as CLIP can
compare an image to text prompts, so they can classify into new label
names without task-specific fine-tuning.

The basic idea is to compare an image embedding \(z_x\) with a text
embedding \(t_k\) for each candidate label prompt:

\[
\hat{p}(y=k \mid x) = \mathrm{softmax}(\alpha \cdot z_x^T t_k)
\]

where \(\alpha\) is a learned or fixed scaling factor. This differs from
the fine-tuned ResNet because the class definitions come from text
prompts rather than from a newly trained classification head. In this
notebook the cell is optional and intentionally low-risk: it uses
\texttt{local\_files\_only=True}, so it runs only if the model is
already cached locally. If the model is not available, the notebook
records that the zero-shot extension was skipped rather than downloading
anything or failing the run.

    If the optional zero-shot cell runs, it compares two different
transfer-learning strategies. The ResNet result is task-specific
transfer: start with ImageNet features and fine-tune on the curated
retail dataset. CLIP-style zero-shot classification is language-guided
transfer: use text prompts to define the target labels without training
on this dataset.

If the cell skips, that is still an intentional result for this notebook
environment. The required project remains reproducible without a network
download, while the section documents how the same problem could be
extended with a state-of-the-art image-text pipeline when the model is
available.

    \subsection{9. Discussion and Paper-Style
Conclusion}\label{discussion-and-paper-style-conclusion}

This notebook compares several approaches to multiclass image
classification on \textbf{Fashion-MNIST} and then extends the analysis
toward more realistic clothing images. The controlled benchmark answers
the first research question: a simple CNN does improve substantially
over classical baselines that use flattened pixels. \textbf{CNN-small}
reaches a clean test macro F1 of \textbf{0.890}, compared with
\textbf{0.824} for \textbf{PCA + Logistic Regression}. The confusion
matrices support this result because the remaining errors concentrate in
visually similar upper-body classes rather than appearing randomly
across all labels.

The robustness experiments make the conclusion stronger. When the
held-out Fashion-MNIST images are perturbed with translations,
rotations, blur, noise, brightness/contrast shifts, and crop-and-pad
distortions, both models lose performance, but the CNN loses much less.
Its average macro F1 drop is \textbf{0.104}, compared with
\textbf{0.431} for the PCA pipeline. This suggests that the CNN's
advantage is not only a clean-benchmark result; it also reflects a
representation that is more stable when image conditions change.

The autoencoder extension adds a second representation-learning result.
Logistic regression trained on learned autoencoder features reaches
\textbf{0.820} clean test macro F1 and has an average shifted-set drop
of \textbf{0.119}. This places it close to PCA on the clean benchmark
but much more robust under perturbation. The statistical reason is that
the autoencoder learns a nonlinear compression of the image
distribution, while PCA keeps only linear directions of high variance.
However, the supervised CNN still performs best because its features are
optimized directly for class discrimination rather than reconstruction.

The real-image sections address the main limitation of Fashion-MNIST: it
is standardized, grayscale, centered, and drawn from a single benchmark
distribution. The curated retail experiment tests whether transfer
learning can adapt the image pipeline to real product photos, and the
best curated ResNet18 run reaches \textbf{0.917} retail test macro F1 on
the six simplified classes. The personal-image holdout then adds a
harder qualitative stress test using phone photos, screenshots, varied
backgrounds, and ambiguous garments. That test shows that the pipeline
can process personal archive-like images, but it also exposes a
remaining domain gap caused by crops, context, multiple garments, and
taxonomy limits.

The final flexibility extensions turn the model into something closer to
a deployable workflow. Selective prediction separates \textbf{coverage}
from \textbf{accepted-image accuracy}, showing why a model should
sometimes abstain and route images to human review. Embedding retrieval
reuses the same ResNet features for nearest-neighbor search, which
supports error diagnosis and recommendation-style workflows without
retraining. The optional zero-shot section documents a second
transfer-learning strategy: image-text models can define classes from
prompts rather than from a fitted classifier head.

Overall, the project supports a more nuanced conclusion than ``CNNs are
better.'' CNNs outperform classical baselines on clean image
classification, and learned visual representations become even more
valuable under distribution shift. Transfer learning can extend those
representations to realistic product photos, but a dependable
archive-level system would still need a larger labeled personal-image
set, clearer outfit-level labels, and a formal abstention policy.

One important limitation is that preprocessing did not simply solve the
real-image problem. Segmentation was attempted, but the masks sometimes
removed meaningful garment parts, so the final retail pipeline uses
raw/full images. That choice avoids broken crops but leaves the model
and the embedding retrieval system exposed to background, pose, and
product-photography cues. In other words, the report's limitation is not
only small sample size; it is also that the learned representation may
mix garment evidence with surrounding visual context.

    \PY{k}{if} \PY{n}{i} \PY{o}{\PYZlt{}} \PY{n+nb}{len}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{steps}\PY{p}{)} \PY{o}{\PYZhy{}} \PY{l+m+mi}{1}\PY{p}{:}

    \subsection{Takeaway}\label{takeaway}

The main result is that \textbf{representation learning matters more as
the data become less standardized}. On clean Fashion-MNIST, CNN-small
outperforms the best classical baseline. Under synthetic distribution
shift, the CNN remains much more stable than PCA + Logistic Regression.
With an autoencoder, even unsupervised learned features transfer better
than linear compression, although they do not match the supervised CNN.

The real-image extension is the most important flexibility improvement.
The project no longer stops at Fashion-MNIST: it fine-tunes
ImageNet-pretrained models on curated retail images, tests the best
model on a small personal real-world holdout, adds confidence-based
abstention, and reuses the ResNet embedding space for retrieval. Because
the personal sample is tiny and some images contain ambiguous or
out-of-taxonomy garments, those results should be interpreted as
qualitative evidence about domain shift rather than a final benchmark.
The strongest next step would be to label a larger personal-image set
and evaluate the same selective-prediction policy on that larger
archive.

    \subsection{10. References}\label{references}

\subsubsection{Core benchmark and baseline
methods}\label{core-benchmark-and-baseline-methods}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Xiao, Han, Kashif Rasul, and Roland Vollgraf. 2017.
  \href{https://arxiv.org/abs/1708.07747}{\emph{Fashion-MNIST: A Novel
  Image Dataset for Benchmarking Machine Learning Algorithms}}. Used as
  the primary controlled image-classification benchmark.
\item
  LeCun, Yann, Leon Bottou, Yoshua Bengio, and Patrick Haffner. 1998.
  \emph{Gradient-Based Learning Applied to Document Recognition}.
  \emph{Proceedings of the IEEE} 86(11): 2278-2324. Used as background
  for convolutional neural networks in image recognition.
\item
  \href{https://pytorch.org/vision/stable/generated/torchvision.datasets.FashionMNIST.html}{torchvision
  FashionMNIST documentation}. Used for dataset loading.
\item
  \href{https://scikit-learn.org/stable/modules/generated/sklearn.dummy.DummyClassifier.html}{scikit-learn
  DummyClassifier documentation}. Used for the majority-class baseline.
\item
  \href{https://scikit-learn.org/stable/modules/generated/sklearn.naive_bayes.GaussianNB.html}{scikit-learn
  GaussianNB documentation}. Used for the Naive Bayes baseline.
\item
  \href{https://scikit-learn.org/stable/modules/generated/sklearn.linear_model.LogisticRegression.html}{scikit-learn
  LogisticRegression documentation}. Used for the linear baseline setup.
\item
  \href{https://scikit-learn.org/stable/modules/generated/sklearn.decomposition.PCA.html}{scikit-learn
  PCA documentation}. Used for the dimensionality-reduction discussion
  and implementation.
\item
  \href{https://scikit-learn.org/stable/modules/generated/sklearn.metrics.f1_score.html}{scikit-learn
  \texttt{f1\_score} documentation}. Used for macro F1 evaluation.
\item
  \href{https://scikit-learn.org/stable/modules/generated/sklearn.model_selection.train_test_split.html}{scikit-learn
  \texttt{train\_test\_split} documentation}. Used for train/validation
  splitting.
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Neural networks, representation learning, and transfer
learning}\label{neural-networks-representation-learning-and-transfer-learning}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \href{https://pytorch.org/tutorials/}{PyTorch Tutorials}. Used for
  implementation guidance on dataset loading and neural-network training
  loops.
\item
  \href{https://pytorch.org/docs/stable/generated/torch.nn.Conv2d.html}{PyTorch
  \texttt{nn.Conv2d} documentation}. Used for the CNN and encoder
  architecture.
\item
  \href{https://pytorch.org/docs/stable/generated/torch.nn.ConvTranspose2d.html}{PyTorch
  \texttt{nn.ConvTranspose2d} documentation}. Used for the decoder
  portion of the autoencoder.
\item
  \href{https://pytorch.org/docs/stable/generated/torch.optim.Adam.html}{PyTorch
  \texttt{torch.optim.Adam} documentation}. Used for CNN and autoencoder
  optimization.
\item
  \href{https://pytorch.org/vision/stable/transforms.html}{torchvision
  transforms functional documentation}. Used for perturbation and
  distribution-shift image transformations.
\item
  Yosinski, Jason, Jeff Clune, Yoshua Bengio, and Hod Lipson. 2014.
  \href{https://papers.nips.cc/paper/5347-how-transferable-are-features-in-deep-neural-networks}{\emph{How
  Transferable Are Features in Deep Neural Networks?}}. Used as
  background for why pretrained visual features can transfer to a new
  clothing-image task.
\item
  Russakovsky, Olga, Jia Deng, Hao Su, Jonathan Krause, Sanjeev
  Satheesh, Sean Ma, Zhiheng Huang, Andrej Karpathy, Aditya Khosla,
  Michael Bernstein, Alexander C. Berg, and Li Fei-Fei. 2015.
  \href{https://arxiv.org/abs/1409.0575}{\emph{ImageNet Large Scale
  Visual Recognition Challenge}}. Used as background for the ImageNet
  pretraining source behind the transfer-learning backbones.
\item
  He, Kaiming, Xiangyu Zhang, Shaoqing Ren, and Jian Sun. 2015.
  \href{https://arxiv.org/abs/1512.03385}{\emph{Deep Residual Learning
  for Image Recognition}}. Used as the architectural reference for
  ResNet18 and the embedding-retrieval extension.
\item
  Howard, Andrew, Mark Sandler, Grace Chu, Liang-Chieh Chen, Bo Chen,
  Mingxing Tan, Weijun Wang, Yukun Zhu, Ruoming Pang, Vijay Vasudevan,
  Quoc V. Le, and Hartwig Adam. 2019.
  \href{https://arxiv.org/abs/1905.02244}{\emph{Searching for
  MobileNetV3}}. Used as the architectural reference for the smaller
  MobileNetV3 transfer-learning comparison.
\item
  \href{https://pytorch.org/vision/stable/models/resnet.html}{torchvision
  ResNet documentation}. Used for the ResNet implementation in
  fine-tuning and embedding retrieval.
\item
  \href{https://pytorch.org/vision/stable/models/mobilenetv3.html}{torchvision
  MobileNetV3 documentation}. Used for the MobileNetV3 implementation in
  the transfer-learning comparison.
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Real-image preprocessing, abstention, retrieval, and
zero-shot
extensions}\label{real-image-preprocessing-abstention-retrieval-and-zero-shot-extensions}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \href{https://huggingface.co/docs/transformers/index}{Hugging Face
  Transformers documentation}. Used by the clothing segmentation and
  optional image-text model pipeline.
\item
  Xie, Enze, Wenhai Wang, Zhiding Yu, Anima Anandkumar, Jose M. Alvarez,
  and Ping Luo. 2021.
  \href{https://arxiv.org/abs/2105.15203}{\emph{SegFormer: Simple and
  Efficient Design for Semantic Segmentation with Transformers}}.
  Provides the segmentation architecture used by the clothes parser
  model.
\item
  Geifman, Yonatan, and Ran El-Yaniv. 2017.
  \href{https://arxiv.org/abs/1705.08500}{\emph{Selective Classification
  for Deep Neural Networks}}. Used as background for the
  confidence-threshold / human-review section, where the model trades
  coverage for accepted-prediction accuracy.
\item
  \href{https://scikit-learn.org/stable/modules/generated/sklearn.metrics.pairwise.cosine_similarity.html}{scikit-learn
  \texttt{cosine\_similarity} documentation}. Used as background for the
  cosine-similarity retrieval metric applied to ResNet embeddings.
\item
  Radford, Alec, Jong Wook Kim, Chris Hallacy, Aditya Ramesh, Gabriel
  Goh, Sandhini Agarwal, Girish Sastry, Amanda Askell, Pamela Mishkin,
  Jack Clark, Gretchen Krueger, and Ilya Sutskever. 2021.
  \href{https://arxiv.org/abs/2103.00020}{\emph{Learning Transferable
  Visual Models From Natural Language Supervision}}. Used as the
  reference for CLIP-style zero-shot image-text classification.
\item
  \href{https://github.com/openai/CLIP}{OpenAI CLIP repository}. Used as
  implementation background for the optional zero-shot image-text
  classification extension.
\end{itemize}
